### Ethnicity

Main article: [Ethnic groups in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_the_Philippines)

See also: [Filipinos](2298105.md), [Pinoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinoy), and [Genetic and anthropology studies on Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_and_anthropology_studies_on_Filipinos)

Another color-coded map

Dominant ethnic groups by province

The Philippines has substantial ethnic diversity, due to foreign influence and the archipelago's division by water and topography.[^cite_note-Banlaoi-2009-303] In the 2020 census, the Philippines' largest ethnic groups were [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_people) (26.0 percent), [Visayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visayans) \[excluding the [Cebuano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebuano_people), [Hiligaynon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiligaynon_people), and [Waray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waray_people)] (14.3 percent), [Ilocano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocano_people) and Cebuano (both eight percent), Hiligaynon (7.9 percent), [Bicolano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicolano_people) (6.5 percent), and Waray (3.8 percent).[^cite_note-PSAGovPH-Ethnicity-2020Census-9] The [country's indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Philippines) consisted of 110 enthnolinguistic groups,[^cite_note-512] with a combined population of 15.56 million, in 2020.[^cite_note-PSAGovPH-Ethnicity-2020Census-9] They include the [Igorot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igorot_people), [Lumad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumad), [Mangyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangyan), and the [indigenous peoples of Palawan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_of_Palawan).[^cite_note-513]&#x20;

[Negritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negrito) are thought to be among the islands' earliest inhabitants.[^cite_note-Dolan-1991-90]<sup>: [35](https://web.archive.org/web/20060927160916/http://countrystudies.us/philippines/35.htm) </sup> These minority aboriginal settlers are an [Australoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australo-Melanesian) group, a remnant of the [first human migration from Africa to Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Dispersal) who were probably displaced by later waves of migration.[^cite_note-514] Some Philippine Negritos have a [Denisovan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisovan) admixture in their [genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genome).[^cite_note-515][^cite_note-516] Ethnic Filipinos generally belong to several Southeast Asian ethnic groups, classified linguistically as [Austronesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_peoples) speaking [Malayo-Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayo-Polynesian_languages).[^cite_note-Ethnologue-PH-517]&#x20;

The Austronesian population's origin is uncertain, but relatives of [Taiwanese aborigines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_indigenous_peoples) probably brought their language and mixed with the region's existing population.[^cite_note-518][^cite_note-519] The Lumad and [Sama-Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sama-Bajau) ethnic groups have an ancestral affinity with the [Austroasiatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austroasiatic_languages) and [Mlabri-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mlabri_language) [Htin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lua_people) peoples of mainland Southeast Asia. Westward expansion of [Papuan ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papuan_languages) from [Papua New Guinea](22943.md) to eastern Indonesia and [Mindanao](20546.md) has been detected in the [Blaan people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaan_people) and the [Sangir language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangir_language), while ancient immigration added some [Indian](14533.md) ancestry to the precolonial [Indianized kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(900%E2%80%931565\)) of the islands.[^cite_note-520]&#x20;

Immigrants arrived in the Philippines from elsewhere in the Spanish Empire, especially [from the Spanish Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_Asian).[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-Mehl-2016-522]<sup>: [Chpt. 6](https://web.archive.org/web/20180614082235/https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/forced-migration-in-the-spanish-pacific-world/unruly-mexicans-in-manila/EF2599210A0715A5A91B23BB9D84B96C) </sup>[^cite_note-Intercolonial-523] A 2016 [National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geno_2.0_Next_Generation) project [concluded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_studies_on_Filipinos) that people living in the Philippine archipelago carried [genetic markers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_marker) in the following percentages: 53 percent [Southeast Asia](28741.md) and [Oceania](22621.md), 36 percent [East Asia](19605700.md), 5 percent [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe), 3 percent [Southern Asia](21566765.md), and 2 percent Native American (from [Latin America](18524.md)).[^cite_note-Mehl-2016-522]<sup>: [Chpt. 6](https://web.archive.org/web/20180614082235/https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/forced-migration-in-the-spanish-pacific-world/unruly-mexicans-in-manila/EF2599210A0715A5A91B23BB9D84B96C) </sup>[^cite_note-524]&#x20;

Descendants of mixed-race couples are known as [Mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Mestizos) or *tisoy*,[^cite_note-525] which during the [Spanish colonial times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1565%E2%80%931898\)), were mostly composed of [Chinese mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangley) (*Mestizos de Sangley*), [Spanish mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Filipino) (*Mestizos de Español*) and the mix thereof (*[tornatrás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torna_atr%C3%A1s)*).[^cite_note-526][^cite_note-527][^cite_note-528] The modern [Chinese Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Filipino) are well-integrated into Filipino society.[^cite_note-Banlaoi-2009-303][^cite_note-529] Primarily the descendants of immigrants from [Fujian](88585.md),[^cite_note-530] the pure ethnic Chinese Filipinos during the [American colonial era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1898%E2%80%931946\)) (early 1900s) purportedly numbered about 1.35 million; while an estimated 22.8 million (around 20 percent) of Filipinos have half or partial Chinese ancestry from precolonial, colonial, and 20th century [Chinese migrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese).[^cite_note-531][^cite_note-senate.gov.ph-532] During the Hispanic era (late 1700s), the tribute-census showed that mixed Spanish Filipinos made up a moderate ratio (around 5 percent) of all citizens.[^cite_note-533]<sup>: 539 </sup>[^cite_note-534]<sup>: 31, 54, 113 </sup> Meanwhile, [a smaller proportion (2.33 percent) of the population were Mexican Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_settlement_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-Intercolonial-523]<sup>: 100 </sup> Almost 300,000 [American citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans_in_the_Philippines) live in the country as of 2023,[^cite_note-535] this increased to 750,000 by year 2025,[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-536] (forming 0.75% of the demographics)[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-536] and up to 250,000 [Amerasians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amerasian),[^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-537] (0.25% of the country), are in the cities of [Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Global_City), [Angeles](842728.md), Manila, and [Olongapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olongapo)[^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-537][^cite_note-538] (When combined, [American](19792942.md)[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-536] and Amerasian descent,[^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-537] form 1% of the population).[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-536][^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-537] Other significant non-indigenous minorities include [Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Filipino)[^cite_note-539] and [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-540] [Japanese Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_in_the_Philippines) include escaped Christians ([Kirishitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirishitan)) who [fled persecutions by Shogun Tokugawa Ieyasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyrs_of_Japan).[^cite_note-541]&#x20;

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_the_Philippines)

*[Ethnologue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnologue)* lists 186 languages for the Philippines, 182 of which are [living languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_language). The other four no longer have any known speakers. Most native languages are part of the [Philippine branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_languages) of the [Malayo-Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayo-Polynesian_languages), which is a branch of the [Austronesian language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages).[^cite_note-Ethnologue-PH-517] Spanish-based [creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language) varieties, collectively known as [Chavacano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavacano), are also spoken.[^cite_note-542] Many [Philippine Negrito languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Negrito_languages#Unique_vocabulary) have unique vocabularies which survived Austronesian acculturation.[^cite_note-543]&#x20;

[Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_language) and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_English) are the country's official languages.[^cite_note-GovPH-OfficialLanguage-7] Filipino, a [standardized version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_language) of [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language), is spoken primarily in Metro Manila.[^cite_note-544] Filipino and English are used in government, education, print, broadcast media, and business, often with a third local language.[^cite_note-Brown-Ganguly-2003-545] [Code-switching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code-switching) between English and other local languages, notably [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taglish), is common.[^cite_note-546] The Philippine constitution provides for [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Spanish) and Arabic on a voluntary, optional basis.[^cite_note-GovPH-OfficialLanguage-7] Spanish, a widely used [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) during the late nineteenth century, [has declined greatly in use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language_in_the_Philippines),[^cite_note-547][^cite_note-548] although Spanish [loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword) are still present in Philippine languages.[^cite_note-549][^cite_note-\:2-550][^cite_note-551] Arabic is primarily taught in [Mindanao](20546.md) Islamic schools.[^cite_note-552]&#x20;

In 2020, the top languages generally spoken at home were Tagalog, [Binisaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binisaya), [Hiligaynon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiligaynon_language), [Ilocano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocano_language), [Cebuano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebuano_language), and [Bikol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bikol_languages).[^cite_note-553] Nineteen [regional languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regional_languages_of_the_Philippines) are auxiliary official languages as media of instruction:[^cite_note-GMA-DepEd-7-Languages-6]&#x20;

- [Aklanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aklanon_language)

- Bikol

- Cebuano

- [Chavacano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavacano)

- Hiligaynon

- [Ibanag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibanag_language)

- Ilocano

- [Ivatan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivatan_language)

- [Kapampangan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapampangan_language)

- [Kinaray-a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karay-a_language)

- [Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maguindanao_language)

- [Maranao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maranao_language)

- [Pangasinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangasinan_language)

- [Sambal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambal_language)

- [Surigaonon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surigaonon_language)

- Tagalog

- [Tausug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tausug_language)

- [Waray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waray_language)

- [Yakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakan_language)

Other indigenous languages, including [Cuyonon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuyonon_language), [Ifugao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifugao_language), [Itbayat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itbayat_language), [Kalinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalinga_language), [Kamayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamayo_language), [Kankanaey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kankanaey_language), [Masbateño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masbate%C3%B1o_language), [Romblomanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romblomanon_language), [Manobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manobo_languages), and several [Visayan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visayan_languages), are used in their respective provinces.[^cite_note-Ethnologue-PH-517] [Filipino Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Sign_Language) is the national sign language, and the language of [deaf education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deafness_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-554]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_Philippines)

Large crowd outside a colorfully-decorated church

Catholics attend Mass at [Basilica del Santo Niño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_del_Santo_Ni%C3%B1o) during the annual [Sinulog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinulog) festival in [Cebu](160278.md).

Although the Philippines is a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_the_Philippines) with [freedom of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_the_Philippines), an overwhelming majority of Filipinos [consider religion very important](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Importance_of_religion_by_country)[^cite_note-555] and [irreligion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_the_Philippines) is very low.[^cite_note-556][^cite_note-557][^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2015Census-Religion-558] [Christianity](8276776.md) is the dominant religion[^cite_note-559][^cite_note-StateGov-ReligiousFreedom-2015-560] followed by about 89 percent of the population.[^cite_note-561] The country had the [world's third-largest Roman Catholic population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country) as of 2013, and was Asia's [largest Christian nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Asia).[^cite_note-562]&#x20;

Census data from [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Philippine_census) found that 78.8 percent of the population professed [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-Catholic-2020Census-563][^cite_note-564] The Catholic church in the Philippines is unique for being by granted by the [Roman Pontiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Pontiff), the [Cerulean Indult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerulean_Indult), the privilege to wear the liturgical color of [Cerulean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerulean) or [Celeste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_blue), in honor of the [Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immaculate_Conception) of Mary, a privilege, not shared by most other Latin-Rite Catholics, except those under the ambit of the Spanish Empire[^cite_note-565] where it also doubly serves as the color of [Native American Royalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turquoise#Cultural_associations) even though the indult had initially fallen out of use due to wars of independence against Spain, the primary supporter of the Immaculate Conception, but was restored to all former Spanish-territories following the First Provincial Council of Manila[^cite_note-566] as the Philippines earned it through reverting to, or staying loyal towards Catholicism, even though the church was divided on the issue of Philippine independence, amidst the schisms produced by the [Philippine Independent Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Independent_Church) as well as other break away Protestant and [Masonic influenced churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freemasonry_in_the_Philippines), after Spanish Colonialism was replaced by the American Imperialism.[^cite_note-567][^cite_note-568] Other [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations_in_the_Philippines) include [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md), the [Philippine Independent Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Independent_Church), and [Seventh-day Adventism](28632.md).[^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Census-Religion-569] [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_Philippines) made up about 5% to 7% of the population in 2010.[^cite_note-570][^cite_note-571] The Philippines sends many [Christian missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mission) around the world, and is a training center for foreign priests and nuns.[^cite_note-572][^cite_note-573]&#x20;

[Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_Philippines) is the country's second-largest religion, with 6.4 percent of the population in the 2020 census.[^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Census-Religion-569] Most Muslims live in Mindanao and nearby islands,[^cite_note-StateGov-ReligiousFreedom-2015-560] and most adhere to the [Shafi'i school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi%27i_school) of [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam).[^cite_note-574]&#x20;

About 0.2 percent of the population [follow indigenous religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Philippine_folk_religions),[^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Census-Religion-569] whose practices and folk beliefs are often [syncretized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_syncretism) with Christianity and Islam.[^cite_note-Rodell-2002-246]<sup>: [29–30](https://books.google.com/books?id=y1CVR74_KHQC\&pg=PA29) </sup>[^cite_note-575] [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_Philippines) is practiced by about 0.04% of the population,[^cite_note-PSAGovPH-2020Census-Religion-569] primarily by Filipinos of Chinese descent.[^cite_note-576]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_the_Philippines)

A steadily-rising graph until the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020

Life expectancy in the Philippines, 1938–2021

[Health care in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_the_Philippines) is provided by the national and local governments, although private payments account for most healthcare spending.[^cite_note-DOH-2018-501]<sup>: 25–27 </sup>[^cite_note-577] Per-capita health expenditure in 2024 was [₱](227672.md)12,751 and health expenditures were 5.9 percent of the country's GDP.[^cite_note-578] The 2023 budget allocation for healthcare was ₱334.9 billion.[^cite_note-OneNews-MarcosSigns2023Budget-579] The 2019 enactment of the [Universal Health Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) by President Duterte facilitated the automatic enrollment of all Filipinos in the [national health insurance program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Health_Insurance_Corporation).[^cite_note-580][^cite_note-581] Since 2018, [Malasakit Centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malasakit_Center) (one-stop shops) have been set up in several government-operated hospitals to provide medical and financial assistance to indigent patients.[^cite_note-582]&#x20;

Average life expectancy in the Philippines as of 2023 is 70.48 years (66.97 years for males, and 74.15 years for females).[^cite_note-CIAWorldFactBook-15] Access to medicine has improved due to increasing Filipino acceptance of [generic drugs](293885.md).[^cite_note-DOH-2018-501]<sup>: 58 </sup> The country's leading causes of death in 2021 were [ischaemic heart diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ischaemic_heart_diseases), [cerebrovascular diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerebrovascular_disease), [COVID-19](63030231.md), [neoplasms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplasm), and [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes).[^cite_note-583] [Communicable diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communicable_disease) are correlated with natural disasters, primarily floods.[^cite_note-584] One million Filipinos have active [tuberculosis](30653.md), the fourth highest global prevalence rate.[^cite_note-585]&#x20;

The Philippines has 1,387 [hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_the_Philippines), 33 percent of which are government-run; 23,281 barangay health stations, 2,592 rural health units, 2,411 [birthing homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birthing_center), and 659 infirmaries provide primary care throughout the country.[^cite_note-RVONL-HealthCare-2021-586] Since 1967, the Philippines had become the largest global supplier of nurses;[^cite_note-587] seventy percent of [nursing graduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nursing_in_the_Philippines) go overseas to work, causing problems in retaining skilled practitioners.[^cite_note-588]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_Philippines)

Further information: [Higher education in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_the_Philippines)

Front of a very old building

Founded in 1611, the [University of Santo Tomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas) is Asia's oldest extant university.

[^cite_note-589]

Primary and secondary schooling in the Philippines consists of six years of elementary period, four years of junior high school, and two years of senior high school.[^cite_note-590] Public education, provided by the government, is free at the elementary and secondary levels and at [most public higher-education institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Access_to_Quality_Tertiary_Education_Act).[^cite_note-OECD-2017May-591][^cite_note-592] [Science high schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Science_High_School_System) for talented students were established in 1963.[^cite_note-593] The government provides technical-vocational training and development through the [Technical Education and Skills Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_Education_and_Skills_Development_Authority).[^cite_note-594] In 2004, the government began offering [alternative education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_Learning_System_\(Philippines\)) to out-of-school children, youth, and adults to improve literacy;[^cite_note-595][^cite_note-596] [madaris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrasa) were mainstreamed in 16 regions that year, primarily in Mindanao Muslim areas under the [Department of Education](5464548.md).[^cite_note-597] [Catholic schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_universities_and_colleges_in_the_Philippines), which number more than 1,500,[^cite_note-598] and higher education institutions are an integral part of the educational system.[^cite_note-599]&#x20;

The Philippines has [1,975 higher education institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_the_Philippines) as of 2019, of which 246 are public and 1,729 are private.[^cite_note-600] Public universities are non-sectarian, and are primarily classified as [state-administered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_state_schools,_colleges_and_universities_in_the_Philippines) or [local government-funded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_colleges_and_universities_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-601][^cite_note-602] The [national university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_university) is the eight-school [University of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines) (UP) system.[^cite_note-603] The country's top-ranked universities are the [University of the Philippines Diliman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines_Diliman), [Ateneo de Manila University](330339.md), [De La Salle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_La_Salle_University), and [University of Santo Tomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas).[^cite_note-604][^cite_note-605][^cite_note-606]&#x20;

In 2019, the Philippines had a basic [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy) rate of 93.8 percent of those five years old or older,[^cite_note-607] and a [functional literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Functional_illiteracy) rate of 91.6 percent of those aged 10 to 64.[^cite_note-608] Education, a significant proportion of the national budget, was allocated ₱900.9 billion from the ₱5.268 trillion 2023 budget.[^cite_note-OneNews-MarcosSigns2023Budget-579] As of 2023, the country has 1,640 [public libraries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_libraries) affiliated with the [National Library of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-609]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Philippines)

A terraced hillside, seen from above

The [Banaue Rice Terraces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banaue_Rice_Terraces), carved by ancestors of the [Ifugao people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifugao_people)

The Philippines has significant cultural diversity, reinforced by the country's fragmented geography.[^cite_note-Junker-1999-46]<sup>: [61](https://books.google.com/books?id=r50BEAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA61) </sup>[^cite_note-Bankoff-2017-610] Spanish and American cultures profoundly influenced Filipino culture as a result of long colonization.[^cite_note-Edelstein-2011-611][^cite_note-Banlaoi-2009-303] The cultures of [Lumads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumad) of Mindanao and the [Sulu Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Archipelago) developed distinctly, since they had limited Spanish influence and more influence from nearby Islamic regions.[^cite_note-Wernstedt-1967-59]<sup>: [503](https://books.google.com/books?id=6Pn0Pfh1Cl0C\&pg=PA503) </sup> Indigenous groups such as the [Igorots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igorot) have preserved their precolonial customs and traditions by [resisting the Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igorot_resistance_to_Spanish_colonization).[^cite_note-612][^cite_note-613] A [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_nationalism) [emerged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_revival) during the 19th century, however, with [shared national symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_the_Philippines) and cultural and historical [touchstones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touchstone_\(metaphor\)).[^cite_note-Bankoff-2017-610]&#x20;

[Hispanic legacies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_influence_on_Filipino_culture) includes the dominance of Catholicism,[^cite_note-Guillermo-2012-67]<sup>: [5](https://books.google.com/books?id=wmgX9M_yETIC\&pg=PA5) </sup>[^cite_note-Edelstein-2011-611] a [creole language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavacano),[^cite_note-614] numerous [loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_loanwords_in_the_Tagalog_language#Spanish),[^cite_note-\:2-550] the endangered [Philippine Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Spanish),[^cite_note-615] arts,[^cite_note-\:3-616] architecture,[^cite_note-\:4-617] [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_literature_in_Spanish),[^cite_note-618] drama,[^cite_note-619] food,[^cite_note-influ1-620] music,[^cite_note-621] dance,[^cite_note-622] fashion[^cite_note-Coo_2014-623] and the prevalence of [Spanish names and surnames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cat%C3%A1logo_alfab%C3%A9tico_de_apellidos), which resulted from an 1849 edict ordering the systematic distribution of family names and the implementation of [Spanish naming customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_naming_customs);[^cite_note-Boquet-2017-225]<sup>: [75](https://books.google.com/books?id=90C4DgAAQBAJ\&pg=PA75) </sup>[^cite_note-Woods-2006-66]<sup>: [237](https://books.google.com/books?id=2Z-n_kDTxf0C\&pg=PT262) </sup> the [names of many locations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_place_names_of_Spanish_origin) also have Spanish origins.[^cite_note-624] American influence on modern Filipino culture[^cite_note-Banlaoi-2009-303] is evident in the use of English,[^cite_note-Nadal-2011-625]<sup>: [12](https://books.google.com/books?id=zN7-s84jAkoC\&pg=PA12) </sup> and Filipino consumption of [fast food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_food) and American films and music.[^cite_note-Edelstein-2011-611]&#x20;

[Public holidays in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_the_Philippines) are classified as regular or special.[^cite_note-626] [Festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_festivals_in_the_Philippines) are primarily religious, and most towns and villages have such a festival (usually to honor a [patron saint](68055.md)).[^cite_note-627][^cite_note-Wendt-1998-628] Better-known festivals include [Ati-Atihan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ati-Atihan_festival),[^cite_note-629] [Dinagyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinagyang),[^cite_note-630] [Moriones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriones_Festival),[^cite_note-631] [Sinulog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinulog),[^cite_note-632] and [Flores de Mayo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flores_de_Mayo)—a month-long devotion to the [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary) held in May.[^cite_note-633] The country's [Christmas season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_in_the_Philippines) begins as early as September 1,[^cite_note-Rood-2019-634]<sup>: [149](https://books.google.com/books?id=JmCpDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA149) </sup> and [Holy Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Week_in_the_Philippines) is a solemn religious observance for its Christian population.[^cite_note-635][^cite_note-Rood-2019-634]<sup>: [149](https://books.google.com/books?id=JmCpDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA149) </sup>&#x20;

### Values

Main article: [Filipino values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_values)

Further information: [Filipino psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_psychology)

Colored outdoor statue of a child pressing their forehead on the hand of a seated elder

Statue in [Iriga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iriga) commemorating *[mano po](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mano_\(gesture\))*

Filipino [values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_\(ethics_and_social_sciences\)) are rooted primarily in personal alliances based in [kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_kinship), obligation, friendship, religion (particularly Christianity), and commerce.[^cite_note-Dolan-1991-90]<sup>: [41](https://web.archive.org/web/20060927161138/https://countrystudies.us/philippines/41.htm) </sup> They center around social harmony through *[pakikisama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakikisama)*,[^cite_note-Qiu-2004-636]<sup>: [74](https://books.google.com/books?id=puYpTx1gE40C\&pg=PA74) </sup> motivated primarily by the desire for acceptance by a group.[^cite_note-637][^cite_note-Warner-2014-638][^cite_note-Nadal-2011-625]<sup>: [47](https://books.google.com/books?id=zN7-s84jAkoC\&pg=PA47) </sup> Reciprocity through *[utang na loob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utang_na_loob)* (a debt of gratitude) is a significant Filipino cultural trait, and an internalized debt can never be fully repaid.[^cite_note-Qiu-2004-636]<sup>: [76](https://books.google.com/books?id=puYpTx1gE40C\&pg=PA76) </sup>[^cite_note-639] The main sanction for divergence from these values are the concepts of *hiya* (shame)[^cite_note-640] and loss of *amor propio* ([self-esteem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-esteem)).[^cite_note-Warner-2014-638]&#x20;

The family is central to Philippine society; norms such as loyalty, maintaining close relationships and care for elderly parents are ingrained in Philippine society.[^cite_note-641][^cite_note-642] Respect for authority and the elderly is valued, and is shown with gestures such as *mano* and the [honorifics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_styles_and_honorifics) *po* and *opo* and *kuya* (older brother) or *ate* (older sister).[^cite_note-643][^cite_note-644] Other Filipino values are optimism about the future, pessimism about the present, concern about other people, friendship and friendliness, [hospitality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitality), religiosity, respect for oneself and others (particularly [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Philippines)), and integrity.[^cite_note-645]&#x20;

### Art and architecture

Main articles: [Arts in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_in_the_Philippines) and [Architecture of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_the_Philippines)

Philippine art combines indigenous folk art, east-Asian and [Classical traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_art).[^cite_note-646][^cite_note-McFerson-2002-647][^cite_note-\:3-616] During the Spanish colonial period, art was used to spread Catholicism mainly through paintings and sculptures. The first recorded sculptor in the Philippines is Juan de los Santos (1590 –1660) known for making retablos.[^cite_note-\:1-648] In 1821, [Damian Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dami%C3%A1n_Domingo), the father of Filipino paintings, opened the [*Academia de Dibujo*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_de_Dibujo_y_Pintura) art school in Binondo.[^cite_note-\:1-648][^cite_note-649] Other artists during Spanish colonial rule included painters such as Josef Luciano Dans, [Jose Honorato Lozano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Honorato_Lozano), Mariano Asuncion[^cite_note-650] and sculptors such as [Isabelo Tampinco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabelo_Tampinco) and Crispulo Hocson.[^cite_note-\:1-648] Artist whose works drew attention to the Philippines are [Juan Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Luna) and [Félix Resurrección Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Resurrecci%C3%B3n_Hidalgo).[^cite_note-651] [Fernando Amorsolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Amorsolo) dominated Philippine paintings during the American colonial period, popular for his [pastoral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoral) scenes of Philippine countryside.[^cite_note-NCCA-652][^cite_note-653] [Victorio Edades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) known as the father of Modern Philippine Art, popularized [Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernism) in the Philippines in 1920s and 1930s.[^cite_note-654]&#x20;

Traditional Philippine architecture has two main models: the indigenous *[bahay kubo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_kubo)* and the *[bahay na bato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_na_bato)*, which developed under Spanish rule.[^cite_note-Boquet-2017-225]<sup>: [438–444](https://books.google.com/books?id=90C4DgAAQBAJ\&pg=PA438) </sup> Some regions, such as [Batanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batanes), differ slightly due to climate; limestone was used as a building material, and houses were built to withstand typhoons.[^cite_note-655][^cite_note-656]&#x20;



[Zamboanga City](25167165.md) [Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamboanga_City_Hall)

[^cite_note-bm-657]

[Spanish architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_architecture) left an imprint in town designs around a [central square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poblacion) or *plaza mayor*, but many of its buildings were damaged or destroyed during World War II.[^cite_note-\:4-617][^cite_note-Ring-1996-57] [Several](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Baroque_churches_in_the_Philippines) Philippine [churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_churches_in_the_Philippines) adapted [baroque architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) to withstand earthquakes, leading to the development of [Earthquake Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_Baroque);[^cite_note-658][^cite_note-Girard-2021-659] [four baroque churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_Churches_of_the_Philippines) have been listed as a collective UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-UNESCO-BaroqueChurches-660] [Spanish colonial fortifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonial_fortifications_in_the_Philippines) (*fuerzas*) in several parts of the Philippines were primarily designed by missionary architects and built by Filipino stonemasons.[^cite_note-661] [Vigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigan), in [Ilocos Sur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocos_Sur), is known for its Hispanic-style houses and buildings.[^cite_note-662]&#x20;

American rule introduced new architectural styles in the construction of government buildings and [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco) theaters.[^cite_note-NCCAGovPH-Architecture-663] During the American period, construction of [Gabaldon school buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabaldon_school_buildings) began,[^cite_note-664] and some city planning using architectural designs and master plans by [Daniel Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Burnham) was done in portions of [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burnham_Plan_of_Manila) and [Baguio](162480.md).[^cite_note-665][^cite_note-666] Part of the Burnham plan was the construction of government buildings reminiscent of [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_architecture) or [Neoclassical architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture).[^cite_note-NCCAGovPH-Architecture-663][^cite_note-Girard-2021-659] Buildings from the Spanish and American periods can be seen in [Iloilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iloilo), especially in [Calle Real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calle_Real,_Iloilo).[^cite_note-667]&#x20;

### Music and dance

Main articles: [Music of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_the_Philippines) and [Dance in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_in_the_Philippines)

Female dancers in colorful dresses

[Tinikling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinikling), a dance depicting the swift leg movements of a *tikling* bird eluding a farmer's traps

[^cite_note-668]

There are two types of Philippine [folk dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_dance), stemming from traditional indigenous influences and Spanish influence.[^cite_note-Rodell-2002-246]<sup>: [173](https://books.google.com/books?id=y1CVR74_KHQC\&pg=PA173) </sup> Although native dances had become less popular,[^cite_note-Villaruz-2006-669]<sup>: [77](https://books.google.com/books?id=qLQOd-PFxe4C\&pg=PA77) </sup> folk dancing began to revive during the 1920s.[^cite_note-Villaruz-2006-669]<sup>: [82](https://books.google.com/books?id=qLQOd-PFxe4C\&pg=PA82) </sup> The [Cariñosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cari%C3%B1osa), a Hispanic Filipino dance, is unofficially considered the country's national dance.[^cite_note-670] Popular indigenous dances include the [Tinikling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinikling) and [Singkil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singkil_\(Maranao_dance\)), which include the rhythmic clapping of bamboo poles.[^cite_note-671][^cite_note-672] Present-day dances vary from delicate [ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballet)[^cite_note-673] to street-oriented [breakdancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakdancing).[^cite_note-674][^cite_note-675]&#x20;

[Rondalya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rondalla) music, with traditional [mandolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandolin)-type [instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_musical_instruments), was popular during the Spanish era.[^cite_note-Thompson-Batalla-2018-172]<sup>: [327](https://books.google.com/books?id=DmkPEAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA327) </sup>[^cite_note-KCET-MasterRondalla-676] Spanish-influenced musicians are primarily [bandurria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandurria)-based bands with 14-string guitars.[^cite_note-677][^cite_note-KCET-MasterRondalla-676] [Kundiman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kundiman) developed during the 1920s and 1930s.[^cite_note-Ellingham-1999-678] The American colonial period exposed many Filipinos to [U.S. culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States) and [popular music](171080.md).[^cite_note-Ellingham-1999-678] [Rock music](25423.md) was introduced to Filipinos during the 1960s and developed into Filipino rock (or [Pinoy rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinoy_rock)), a term encompassing [pop rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_rock), [alternative rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_rock), [heavy metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_metal_music), [punk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_rock), [new wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_wave_music), [ska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ska), and [reggae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reggae). Martial law in the 1970s produced [Filipino folk rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_folk_music) bands and artists who [were at the forefront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protest_music_against_the_Marcos_dictatorship) of political demonstrations.[^cite_note-Murray-2016-679]<sup>: [38–41](https://books.google.com/books?id=ot2uDAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA38) </sup> The decade also saw the birth of the [Manila sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_sound) and [Original Pilipino Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_Pilipino_Music) (OPM).[^cite_note-Shunwei-2022-680][^cite_note-Woods-2006-66]<sup>: [171](https://books.google.com/books?id=2Z-n_kDTxf0C\&pg=PT196) </sup> [Filipino hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_hip-hop), which originated in 1979, entered the mainstream in 1990.[^cite_note-681][^cite_note-Murray-2016-679]<sup>: [38–41](https://books.google.com/books?id=ot2uDAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA38) </sup> [Karaoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karaoke) is also popular.[^cite_note-682] From 2010 to 2020, [Pinoy pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinoy_pop) (P-pop) was influenced by [K-pop](629945.md) and [J-pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J-pop).[^cite_note-683]&#x20;

Locally produced theatrical drama became established during the late 1870s. Spanish influence around that time introduced *[zarzuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarzuela)* plays (with music)[^cite_note-Liu-2016-684] and *[comedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comedia_\(play\))*&#x73;, with dance. The plays became popular throughout the country,[^cite_note-Villaruz-2006-669]<sup>: [69–70](https://books.google.com/books?id=qLQOd-PFxe4C\&pg=PA69) </sup> and were written in a number of local languages.[^cite_note-Liu-2016-684] American influence introduced [vaudeville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaudeville) and ballet.[^cite_note-Villaruz-2006-669]<sup>: [69–70](https://books.google.com/books?id=qLQOd-PFxe4C\&pg=PA69) </sup> [Realistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(theatre\)) theater became dominant during the 20th century, with plays focusing on contemporary political and social issues.[^cite_note-Liu-2016-684]&#x20;

### Literature

Main article: [Philippine literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_literature)

photograph of José Rizal

José Rizal's writings inspired the [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution).

Philippine literature consists of [works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipiniana) usually written in Filipino, [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_literature_in_Spanish), or [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_literature_in_English). Some of the earliest well-known works were created from the 17th to the 19th centuries.[^cite_note-685] They include *[Ibong Adarna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibong_Adarna)*, an epic about an eponymous magical bird,[^cite_note-686] and *[Florante at Laura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florante_at_Laura)* by Tagalog author [Francisco Balagtas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Balagtas).[^cite_note-687][^cite_note-688] [José Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Rizal) wrote the novels *[Noli Me Tángere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noli_Me_T%C3%A1ngere_\(novel\))* and *[El filibusterismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_filibusterismo)*,[^cite_note-689] both of which depict the injustices of Spanish colonial rule.[^cite_note-690]&#x20;

[Folk literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_folk_literature) was relatively unaffected by colonial influence until the 19th century due to Spanish indifference. Most printed literary works during Spanish colonial rule were religious in nature, although [Filipino elites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilustrado) who later learned Spanish wrote [nationalistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_nationalism) literature.[^cite_note-Rodell-2002-246]<sup>: [59–62](https://books.google.com/books?id=y1CVR74_KHQC\&pg=PA59) </sup> The American arrival [began](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_the_Philippines_during_American_rule) Filipino literary use of English[^cite_note-Rodell-2002-246]<sup>: [65–66](https://books.google.com/books?id=y1CVR74_KHQC\&pg=PA65) </sup> and influenced the development of the [Philippine comics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_comics) industry that flourished from the 1920s through the 1970s.[^cite_note-691][^cite_note-692] In the late 1960s, during the presidency of Ferdinand Marcos, Philippine literature was [influenced by political activism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_activism_in_the_Philippines_\(1965%E2%80%931972\)); many poets began using Tagalog, in keeping with the country's oral traditions.[^cite_note-Rodell-2002-246]<sup>: [69–71](https://books.google.com/books?id=y1CVR74_KHQC\&pg=PA69) </sup>&#x20;

[Philippine mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_mythology) has been handed down primarily through oral tradition;[^cite_note-693] popular [figures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_mythological_figures) are [Maria Makiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Makiling),[^cite_note-694] [Lam-ang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biag_ni_Lam-Ang),[^cite_note-695] and the [Sarimanok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarimanok).[^cite_note-Rodell-2002-246]<sup>: [61](https://books.google.com/books?id=y1CVR74_KHQC\&pg=PA61) </sup>[^cite_note-696] The country has a number of [folk epics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_epic_poetry).[^cite_note-697] Wealthy families could preserve transcriptions of the epics as family heirlooms, particularly in Mindanao; the [Maranao-language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maranao_language) *[Darangen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darangen)* is an example.[^cite_note-698]&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Mass media in the Philippines](16602780.md)

Further information: [Cinema of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_the_Philippines)

TV network logo, a multicolored triangle

People's Television Network logo

Philippine media primarily uses Filipino and English, although [broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting) has shifted to Filipino.[^cite_note-Brown-Ganguly-2003-545] [Television shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_television_shows), commercials, and [films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_Philippine_films) are regulated by the [Movie and Television Review and Classification Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movie_and_Television_Review_and_Classification_Board).[^cite_note-699][^cite_note-700] Most Filipinos obtain news and information from television, the Internet,[^cite_note-701] and [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_use_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-CNNPH-SWSFacebook-702] The country's flagship state-owned broadcast-television network is the [People's Television Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Television_Network) (PTV).[^cite_note-703] [ABS-CBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABS-CBN) and [GMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GMA_Network), both [free-to-air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free-to-air), were the dominant TV networks;[^cite_note-BBC-Philippines-Profile-704] before the May 2020 [Philippine government denial of ABS-CBN's franchise renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABS-CBN_franchise_renewal_controversy), it was the country's largest network.[^cite_note-705] [Philippine television dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_television_drama), known as *teleserye*s and mainly produced by [ABS-CBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ABS-CBN_original_drama_series) and [GMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_GMA_Network_original_drama_series), are also seen in several other countries.[^cite_note-706][^cite_note-707]&#x20;

Local film-making [began in 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_films_before_1940) with the release of the first Filipino-produced [feature film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_film): *[Dalagang Bukid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalagang_Bukid)* (*A Girl from the Country*), directed by [Jose Nepomuceno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Nepomuceno).[^cite_note-Armes-1987-128][^cite_note-Tofighian-2006-129]<sup>: 8 </sup> [Production companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_film_studios) remained small during the [silent film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_film) era, but sound films and larger productions emerged in 1933. The postwar 1940s to the early 1960s are considered a high point for Philippine cinema. The 1962–1971 decade saw a decline in quality films, although the commercial film industry expanded until the 1980s.[^cite_note-Armes-1987-128] Critically acclaimed Philippine films include *[Himala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himala)* (*Miracle*) and *[Oro, Plata, Mata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oro,_Plata,_Mata)* (*Gold, Silver, Death*), both released in 1982.[^cite_note-708][^cite_note-709] Since the turn of the 21st century, the country's film industry has struggled to compete with larger-budget foreign films[^cite_note-710] (particularly [Hollywood films](418334.md)).[^cite_note-711][^cite_note-712] [Art films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_film) have thrived, however, and several [indie films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_New_Wave) have been successful domestically and abroad.[^cite_note-Forbes-CinemaGrowingFast-713][^cite_note-714][^cite_note-715]&#x20;

The Philippines has a large number of [radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_the_Philippines) and [newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-BBC-Philippines-Profile-704] English [broadsheets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadsheet) are popular among executives, professionals and students.[^cite_note-Thompson-2003-132]<sup>: [233–251](https://books.google.com/books?id=W1h9oF9rj-MC\&pg=PA233) </sup> Less-expensive Tagalog [tabloids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabloid_\(newspaper_format\)), which grew during the 1990s, are popular (particularly in Manila);[^cite_note-716] however, overall newspaper readership is declining in favor of [online news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_as_a_news_source).[^cite_note-CNNPH-SWSFacebook-702][^cite_note-717] The top three newspapers, by nationwide readership and credibility,[^cite_note-Thompson-2003-132]<sup>: [233](https://books.google.com/books?id=1VI9AAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA233) </sup> are the *[Philippine Daily Inquirer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Daily_Inquirer)*, *[Manila Bulletin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bulletin)*, and *[The Philippine Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Philippine_Star)*.[^cite_note-718][^cite_note-719] Although [freedom of the press](105323.md) is protected by the constitution,[^cite_note-720] the country was listed as the seventh-most-dangerous country for journalists in 2022 by the [Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists) due to 13 unsolved murders of journalists.[^cite_note-721]&#x20;

The Philippine population are the world's top Internet users.[^cite_note-722] In early 2021, 67 percent of Filipinos (73.91 million) had Internet access; the overwhelming majority used [smartphones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartphone).[^cite_note-723]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Filipino cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_cuisine)

Chunky soup in a white bowl

A bowl of fish *[sinigang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinigang)*

From its Malayo-Polynesian origins, traditional Philippine cuisine has evolved since the 16th century. It was primarily influenced by Hispanic, [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Chinese_cuisine), and [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_cuisine) cuisines, which were adapted to the Filipino palate.[^cite_note-724][^cite_note-725] Filipinos tend to prefer robust flavors,[^cite_note-726] centered on sweet, salty, and sour combinations.[^cite_note-Aquino-Porter-2022-727]<sup>: [88](https://books.google.com/books?id=ldCPEAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA88) </sup> Regional variations exist throughout the country; rice is the general staple [starch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starch#Food)[^cite_note-728] but [cassava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassava-based_dishes) is more common in parts of Mindanao.[^cite_note-729][^cite_note-730] [Adobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_adobo) is the unofficial national dish.[^cite_note-731] Other [popular dishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_dishes) include *[lechón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lech%C3%B3n)*, *[kare-kare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kare-kare)*, *[sinigang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinigang)*,[^cite_note-732] *[pancit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pancit)*, *[lumpia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumpia)*, and *[arroz caldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arroz_caldo)*.[^cite_note-Anderson-2018-733][^cite_note-734][^cite_note-735] [Traditional desserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_desserts) are *kakanin* ([rice cakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_cake)), which include *[puto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puto_\(food\))*, *[suman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suman_\(food\))*, and *[bibingka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibingka)*.[^cite_note-736][^cite_note-737] Ingredients such as [calamansi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamansi),[^cite_note-738] *[ube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ube_halaya)*,[^cite_note-739] and *[pili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canarium_ovatum)* are used in Filipino desserts.[^cite_note-740][^cite_note-741] The generous use of [condiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_condiments) such as *[patis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patis_\(sauce\))*, *[bagoong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagoong)*, and *[toyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy_sauce#Filipino)* impart a distinctive Philippine flavor.[^cite_note-Anderson-2018-733][^cite_note-Aquino-Porter-2022-727]<sup>: [73](https://books.google.com/books?id=ldCPEAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA73) </sup>There are vegan filipino food and they include laing, lumpiang gulay, ensaladang talong, turon, ginataang bilo-bilo, biko, espasol, banana cue, camote cue, taho, suman, carioca, and lumpiang sariwa.&#x20;

Unlike other East or Southeast Asian countries, most Filipinos do not eat with [chopsticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chopsticks); they use spoons and forks.[^cite_note-742][^cite_note-743] Traditional eating with the hands or fingers[^cite_note-744] (known as *[kamayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamayan)*) had been used in less urbanized areas,[^cite_note-745]<sup>: [266–268, 277](https://books.google.com/books?id=y6vTun3i4NQC\&pg=PA266) </sup> but has been popularized with the introduction of Filipino food to foreigners and city residents.[^cite_note-746][^cite_note-747]&#x20;

### Sports and recreation

Main articles: [Sports in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_the_Philippines) and [Traditional games in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_in_the_Philippines)

Team photo, with each blue-uniformed member wearing a gold medal

The [Philippines men's national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_men%27s_national_basketball_team) celebrating their [2015 Southeast Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_at_the_2015_Southeast_Asian_Games) championship

[Basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_in_the_Philippines), played at the amateur and professional levels, is considered the country's most popular sport.[^cite_note-748][^cite_note-749] Other popular sports include [boxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_boxing_in_the_Philippines) and billiards, boosted by the achievements of [Manny Pacquiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manny_Pacquiao) and [Efren Reyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efren_Reyes).[^cite_note-Rood-2019-634]<sup>: [142](https://books.google.com/books?id=LmmtDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA142) </sup>[^cite_note-750] The national [martial art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_martial_arts) is [Arnis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnis).[^cite_note-751] *Sabong* ([cockfighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cockfight)) is popular entertainment, especially among Filipino men, and was documented by the [Magellan expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magellan_expedition).[^cite_note-752] [Video gaming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Video_games_in_the_Philippines) and [esports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esports_in_the_Philippines) are emerging pastimes,[^cite_note-753][^cite_note-Booc-2019-754] with the popularity of [indigenous games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_in_the_Philippines) such as *[patintero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patintero)*, *[tumbang preso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumbang_preso)*, *[luksong tinik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luksong_tinik)*, and *[piko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piko_\(Filipino_game\))* declining among young people.[^cite_note-755][^cite_note-Booc-2019-754]&#x20;

The [men's national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_national_football_team) has participated in one [Asian Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_AFC_Asian_Cup).[^cite_note-756] The [women's national football team](5514753.md) qualified for the [2023 FIFA Women's World Cup](45271353.md), their first [World Cup](244862.md), in January 2022.[^cite_note-757] The Philippines has participated in every [Summer Olympic Games](27566.md) since [1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_at_the_1924_Summer_Olympics), except when they supported the [American-led boycott of the 1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics_boycott).[^cite_note-758][^cite_note-759] It was the first [tropical nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_nations_at_the_Winter_Olympics) to compete at the [Winter Olympic Games](34069.md), [debuting in 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_at_the_1972_Winter_Olympics).[^cite_note-760][^cite_note-761] In 2021, the Philippines received its first-ever Olympic gold medal with [weightlifter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting) [Hidilyn Diaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hidilyn_Diaz)'s victory in [Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-762]&#x20;

[^cite_note-Unitary-398]: &#x20;["Federal Systems, Intergovernmental Relations and Federated Regions"](https://www.idea.int/sites/default/files/publications/federal-systems-intergovernmental-relations-and-federated-regions.pdf) (PDF). *idea.int*. [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance). November 2019. Retrieved May 26, 2026. The current system of government has been in existence since Spanish colonial rule and the logic behind it then was to facilitate the extraction of goods from the colony (the Philippines) to the colonizing country (Spain). Colonial rule has now been replaced by what is referred to as 'Imperial Manila'. While there have been attempts to address this problem, such as decentralization, most of the interventions have failed since all have been confined within the current unitary form of government.
